A generalization of Deodhar’s defect statistic for Iwahori–Hecke algebras of type 𝖡𝖢 \mathsf{BC}
Gavin Hobbs † , Tommy Parisi † , Mark Skandera † , and Jiayuan Wang †

Abstract
. Let H H be the Iwahori–Hecke algebra corresponding to any Coxeter group. Deodhar’s defect statistic [ Geom. Dedicata 36 , (1990) pp. 95–119] allows one to expand products of simple Kazhdan–Lusztig basis elements of H H in the natural basis of H H . Clearwater and the third author gave a type- 𝖠 \mathsf{A} extension [ Ann. Comb. 25 , no. 3 (2021) pp. 757–787] of this formula which combinatorially describes the natural expansion of products of Kazhdan–Lusztig basis elements indexed by “smooth” elements of the symmetric group. We similarly give a type- 𝖡𝖢 \mathsf{BC} extension of Deodhar’s result which combinatorially describes the natural expansion of Kazhdan–Lusztig basis elements indexed by hyperoctahedral group elements which are “simultaneously smooth” in types 𝖡 \mathsf{B} and 𝖢 \mathsf{C} .



1.
Introduction

Let
(W,S)
be a Coxeter system
with generating set
S=\{s_{1},\dotsc,s_{m}\}
, let
\leq
denote the Bruhat order on
W
, let
H=H(W)
be the Iwahori–Hecke algebra corresponding to
W
, and let
\{T_{w}\,|\,w\in W\}
be the
natural basis
of
H
as a
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\frac{1}{2}},q^{\negthinspace\bar{\,}\frac{1}{2}}]
-module. (See, e.g.,
[
4
]
.)
A second basis
\{C^{\prime}_{w}(q)\,|\,w\in W\}
introduced by Kazhdan and Lusztig
[
13
]
, sometimes rescaled as
\{\widetilde{C}_{w}(q)\,|\,w\in W\}
with
\widetilde{C}_{w}(q):=q^{\ell(w)/2}C^{\prime}_{w}(q)
, is important in many areas of mathematics. Products of these elements expand nonnegatively in the natural and Kazhdan–Lusztig bases, and have appeared in intersection homology
[
1
]
,
[
17
]
, algorithmic and combinatorial description of Kazhdan–Lusztig basis elements themselves
[
3
]
,
[
8
]
, Schubert varieties
[
3
]
, total nonnegativity
[
10
]
,
[
15
]
,
[
18
]
,
[
19
]
,
trace evaluations
[
5
]
,
[
6
]
,
[
11
]
,
[
12
]
,
[
16
]
, and chromatic symmetric functions
[
5
]
,
[
16
]
.

We will focus on Deodhar’s result
[
8
, Prop. 3.5]

concerning sequences
(s_{i_{1}},\dotsc,s_{i_{k}})
of generators in
S
, products of the
corresponding Kazhdan–Lusztig basis elements
\widetilde{C}_{s_{i_{j}}\negthickspace}(q)=T_{e}+T_{s_{i_{j}}}
of and their natural expansions

(1.1)

\widetilde{C}_{s_{i_{1}}\negthickspace}(q)\cdots\widetilde{C}_{s_{i_{k}}\negthickspace}(q)=\sum_{w\in W}a_{w}T_{w}.

Deodhar described the coefficients
\{a_{w}\,|\,w\in W\}\subset\mathbb{Z}[q]

in terms of
subexpressions
of
(s_{i_{1}},\dotsc,s_{i_{k}})
, sequences
\sigma=(\sigma_{1},\dotsc,\sigma_{k})
with
\sigma_{j}\in\{e,s_{i_{j}}\}
for
j=1,\dotsc,k
. Call index
j
a
defect
of
\sigma
if

\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{j-1}s_{i_{j}}<\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{j-1}
and let
\mathrm{dfct}(\sigma)
denote the number of defects of
\sigma
. Each coefficient on the right-hand side of (
1.1
) is given by

(1.2)

a_{w}=\sum_{\sigma}q^{\mathrm{dfct}(\sigma)},

where the sum is over all subexpressions

\sigma
of
(s_{i_{1}},\dotsc,s_{i_{k}})

satisfying
\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{k}=w
. (Our terminology differs a bit from that of Deodhar.)

Billey and Warrington
[
3
, Rmk. 6]

observed that when
W
and
H
are
the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
and type-
\mathsf{A}
Iwahori–Hecke algebra

H_{n}^{\mathsf{A}}(q)
, the defect statistic has the following simple graphical interpretation.
Let
F=F_{s_{i_{1}}}\circ\cdots\circ F_{s_{i_{k}}}
be the wiring diagram corresponding to
(s_{i_{1}},\dotsc,s_{i_{k}})
, where
\circ
denotes concatenation, and the factor wiring diagrams

F_{s_{1}},\dotsc,F_{s_{n-1}}

are the planar networks

(1.3)

{}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -7.87 55.12 L 0 47.24 L 7.87 55.12}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -7.87 39.37 L 0 47.24 L 7.87 39.37}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -7.87 7.87 L 7.87 7.87}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -7.87 -7.87 L 7.87 -7.87}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -7.87 -23.62 L 7.87 -23.62}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\negthickspace,

respectively. Edges of
F
are understood to be oriented from left to right, with
n

source

vertices on the left,

n

sink
vertices on the right,
and
k
more
interior
vertices, one per factor. We can cover
F
with
2^{k}
different path families of the form
\pi=(\pi_{1},\dotsc,\pi_{n})
, if we allow two paths meeting at the interior vertex of a factor
F_{s_{i_{j}}}
either to cross or not to cross there. Let
\Pi_{w}(F)
be the subset of these path families
having
type
w
, i.e., for
i=1,\dotsc,n
, path
\pi_{i}
begins at source
i
and terminates at sink
w_{i}
. Call index
j
a
(type-
\mathsf{A}
) defect
of
\pi
if the two paths meeting in
F_{s_{i_{j}}}
cross an odd number of times in
F_{s_{i_{1}}}\circ\cdots\circ F_{s_{i_{j-1}}}
, and let
\mathrm{dfct}(\pi)
be the number
of defects in
\pi
. Then each coefficient on the right-hand side of (
1.1
) is given by

(1.4)

a_{w}=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{w}(F)}q^{\mathrm{dfct}(\pi)}.

Clearwater and the third author
[
6
, Cor. 5.3]
extended this type-
\mathsf{A}
result to products of the form

(1.5)

\widetilde{C}_{v^{(1)}}(q)\cdots\widetilde{C}_{v^{(k)}}(q)=\sum_{w}a_{w}T_{w}

in
H_{n}^{\mathsf{A}}(q)
,
where
v^{(1)},\dotsc,v^{(k)}

are maximal elements of parabolic subgroups of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, and
each factor

\widetilde{C}_{v^{(j)}}(q):=\sum_{u\leq v^{(j)}}\negthickspace T_{u}

belongs to the modified signless Kazhdan–Lusztig basis of
H_{n}^{\mathsf{A}}(q)
.
Again we have (
1.4
), where

F
has the form
F_{v^{(1)}}\circ\cdots\circ F_{v^{(k)}}
,
with factors belonging to a class of planar networks generalizing those in
(
1.3
) in that their interior vertices may have indegree and outdegree greater than
2
. More generally still, the formula (
1.4
) holds if permutations
v^{(1)},\dotsc,v^{(k)}
correspond to smooth type-
\mathsf{A}
Schubert varieties, i.e., if they avoid the patterns
3412
and
4231
.

We extend this result further to analogous products of elements
of the Kazhdan–Lusztig basis of the type-
\mathsf{BC}
Iwahori–Hecke algebra

H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)

in Section
5
.
To do so, we review necessary background material on the hyperoctahedral group in Section
2
,

H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
in Section
3
,
and type-
\mathsf{BC}
planar networks in Section
4
. We discuss
open problems in Section
6
.

2.
The hyperoctahedral group

Recall that for a Coxeter group
W
with generating set
S

and an element
w\in W
, an expression
w=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{\ell}}
for
w
as a product of generators is called
reduced

if it is as short as possible, and
\ell=\ell(w)
is called the
length
of
w
. We define the
Bruhat order
on
W
by
declaring
v\leq w
if some (equivalently, every) reduced expression
for
w
contains a subsequence which is a reduced expression for
v
. By this definition we clearly have

(2.1)

v\leq w\ \Longleftrightarrow\ v^{-1}\leq w^{-1}.

For a subset
J\subseteq S
, let
\smash{W_{J}}
denote the subgroup of
W
generated by
J
, and call such a subgroup
parabolic
.
Each coset
w\smash{W_{J}}
of
W_{J}
has a unique Bruhat-minimal element and, if
W
is finite, a unique Bruhat-maximal element
[
4
, Cor. 2.4.5]
.
Let
\smash{W^{J}_{-}}
denote the set of minimal coset representatives.
It is known
[
4
, Defn. 2.4.2, Lem. 2.4.3]
that we have

(2.2)

W^{J}_{-}=\{w\in W\,|\,ws>w\text{ for all }s\in J\}=\{w\in W\,|\,wv\geq v\text{ for all }v\in W_{J}\}

and that there is a bijection
[
4
, Prop. 2.4.4]

(2.3)

W^{J}_{-}\times W_{J}\;\overset{1-1}{\longleftrightarrow}\;W

given by simple multiplication
(w,u)\mapsto wu
and satisfying
\ell(w)+\ell(u)=\ell(wu)
.
The Bruhat order on
W
induces a related partial order on the set
W/W_{J}
of cosets
\{wW_{J}\,|\,w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\}
. Specifically, we declare
vW_{J}\leq wW_{J}
if elements of the cosets satisfy any of the three (equivalent) inequalities in the Bruhat order on
W

[
9
, Lem. 2.2]
.

(i)

The minimal element of
vW_{J}
is less than
or equal to
the minimal element of
wW_{J}
.

(ii)

The maximal element of
vW_{J}
is less than
or equal to
the maximal element of
wW_{J}
, if these exist.

(iii)

At least one element of
vW_{J}
is less than
or equal to
at least one element of
wW_{J}
.

We call this poset the
Bruhat order on
W/W_{J}
.

The
hyperoctahedral group

\,\mathfrak{B}_{n}
is the Coxeter group of type
\mathsf{B}_{n}=\mathsf{C}_{n}
, with generators
S=\{s_{0},\dotsc,s_{n-1}\}
, and relations

(2.4)

\displaystyle{s_{i}}^{2}

\displaystyle=e

\displaystyle\text{for $i=0,\dotsc,n-1$,}

\displaystyle s_{0}s_{1}s_{0}s_{1}

\displaystyle=s_{1}s_{0}s_{1}s_{0},

\displaystyle s_{i}s_{j}

\displaystyle=s_{j}s_{i}

\displaystyle\text{for $i,j\geq 0$ and $|i-j|\geq 2$,}

\displaystyle s_{i}s_{j}s_{i}

\displaystyle=s_{j}s_{i}s_{j}

\displaystyle\text{for $i,j\geq 1$ and $|i-j|=1$.}

The parabolic subgroup of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
generated by
\{s_{1},\dotsc,s_{n-1}\}
is the
symmetric group

\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, the Coxeter group of type
\mathsf{A}_{n-1}
.

Like elements of the symmetric group, elements of the hyperoctahedral group naturally correspond to permutations of
letters belonging to a certain alphabet.

Specifically, we define the alphabet

[\overline{n},n]:=\{-n,\dotsc,n\}\smallsetminus\{0\}

with notation
\overline{a}:=-a
for all
a\in[\overline{n},n]
,
and we consider permutations
w_{\overline{n}}\cdots w_{\overline{1}}w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
of
[\overline{n},n]
which satisfy
w_{\overline{i}}=\overline{w_{i}}
for all
i\in[\overline{n},n]
. We define a correspondence between
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
and such permutations via the (left) action of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
on these permutations defined by

(1)

s_{0}
swaps the letters in positions
\overline{1}
,
1
,

(2)

s_{i}
(
i=1,\dotsc,n-1
) simultaneously swaps
the letters in positions
i
,
i+1
with each other,
and
the letters in positions
\overline{i}
,
\overline{i+1}
with each other.

Then for
w=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{r}}\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
,
we define the
(long) one-line notation
of
w
to be the permutation

(2.5)

w_{\overline{n}}\cdots w_{\overline{1}}w_{1}\cdots w_{n}=s_{i_{1}}(s_{i_{2}}(\cdots(s_{i_{r}}(\overline{n}\cdots\overline{1}1\cdots n))\cdots)).

For example, when
n=4
, the element
s_{0}s_{1}\in\mathfrak{B}_{4}
has long one-line notation

(2.6)

s_{0}(s_{1}(\overline{4}\overline{3}\overline{2}\overline{1}1234))=s_{0}(\overline{4}\overline{3}\overline{1}\overline{2}2134)=\overline{4}\overline{3}\overline{1}2\overline{2}134.

(By our definition, the right action of
s_{0}
swaps the letters
1,\overline{1}
,
wherever they are, while the right action of
s_{i}
for
i=1,\dotsc,n-1
swaps
the letters
i
,
i+1
, wherever they are, while simultaneously swapping the letters
\overline{i}
,
\overline{i+1}
,
wherever they are.) It follows that
w_{i}^{-1}
is the index
j
satisfying
w_{j}=i
.

The condition
w_{\overline{i}}=\overline{w_{i}}

implies that each element (
2.5
) is completely determined by the subword
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
, called the
short one-line notation
of
w
.

The set of short one-line notations of elements of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
is the set of
signed permutations
of
[1,n]
:
words
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
with letters in the alphabet
[\overline{n},n]

having no repeated absolute values.

The correspondence between
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
and signed permutations is given by the left action of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
on signed permutations defined by

(1)

s_{0}
negates the letter in position
1
, and

(2)

s_{i}
(
i=1,\dotsc,n-1
) swaps the letters in positions
i
,
i+1
with each other.

For example the short one-line notation
\overline{2}134
of the element
s_{0}s_{1}\in\mathfrak{B}_{4}

(
2.6
) can be obtained by its action on
1234
:

(2.7)

s_{0}(s_{1}(1234))=s_{0}(2134)=\overline{2}134.

(By our definition, the right action of
s_{0}
on signed permutations negates the letter
1
or
\overline{1}
, wherever it is, while the right action of
s_{i}
for
i=1,\dotsc,n-1
swaps
the letters having absolute values
i
,
i+1
, wherever they are, while leaving any negation signs in their original positions.)

Some properties of elements of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
can be stated in terms of
pattern avoidance
. Given a word
u=u_{1}\cdots u_{k}
with distinct letters, we say that a subword
w_{i_{1}}\cdots w_{i_{k}}
of
w_{\overline{n}}\cdots w_{\overline{1}}w_{1}\cdots w_{n}

matches the pattern
u
if we have

w_{i_{h}}<w_{i_{j}}\text{ if and only if }u_{h}<u_{j}

for all
1\leq h<j\leq k
.
We say that
w

avoids the pattern
u
if no subword of
w
matches the pattern
u
.

We define an
inversion
of any word
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
to be a pair
(i,j)
of indices satisfying
i<j
and
w_{i}>w_{j}
. Let
\textsc{inv}(w)
denote the number of inversions in
w
. For
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, it is well known that we have
\textsc{inv}(w_{1}\cdots w_{n})=\ell(w)
. For
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
we can similarly compute
\ell(w)
by inspecting the short one-line notation of
w
. (See, e.g.,
[
4
, Prop. 8.1.1, Eqn. (8.2)]
.)

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
have short one-line notation
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
.
Then its length satisfies

\ell(w)=\textsc{inv}(w_{1}\cdots w_{n})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\\
w_{i}<0\end{subarray}}|w_{i}|.

We can also compute
\ell(w)
by counting certain inversions in the long one-line notation of
w
.

Lemma 2.2
.

The length of
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
equals the number of pairs
(i,j)
with
|i|\leq j
and
j
appearing earlier than
i
in

w_{\overline{n}}\cdots w_{\overline{1}}w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
.

Proof.

Let
\ell^{\prime}
be the proposed formula for length, and assume that
\ell^{\prime}
agrees with the true length
\ell
for hyperoctahedral groups
\mathfrak{B}_{1},\dotsc,\mathfrak{B}_{n-1}
. Consider
w=w_{\overline{n}}\cdots w_{\overline{1}}w_{1}\cdots w_{n}\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
satisfying
w_{k}=n
,
w_{\overline{k}}=\overline{n}
for some

k\in[\overline{n},n]
, and define
v=v_{\overline{n-1}}\cdots v_{\overline{1}}v_{1}\cdots v_{n-1}\in\mathfrak{B}_{n-1}

by removing letters
n
,
\overline{n}
from
w
. By Lemma
2.1
we have

(2.8)

\ell(w)=\begin{cases}\ell(v)+n-k&\text{if $k>0$},\\
\ell(v)+|k|-1+n&\text{if $k<0$},\end{cases}

since
n
forms inversions with
w_{k+1},\dotsc,w_{n}
if it appears in position
k>0
, and
\overline{n}
forms inversions with
w_{1}\cdots w_{|k|-1}
if it appears in position
|k|
with
k<0
. On the other hand, by the definition of
\ell^{\prime}
and induction we have

\displaystyle\ell^{\prime}(w)

\displaystyle=\ell^{\prime}(v)+\#\text{ letters following $n$ in $w_{\overline{n}}\cdots w_{\overline{1}}w_{1}\cdots w_{n}$}

\displaystyle=\ell(v)+\#\text{ letters following $n$ in $w_{\overline{n}}\cdots w_{\overline{1}}w_{1}\cdots w_{n}$}.

By (
2.8
), this is
\ell(w)
.
∎

Certain elements of the hyperoctahedral group are most easily defined in terms of subintervals of
[\overline{n},n]
, where we declare any subset

[a,b]:=\{a,\dotsc,b\}\smallsetminus\{0\}

of
[\overline{n},n]

to be
an
interval
, even if
a<0<b
.
Roughly speaking, we define a
reversal
of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
to be an element
s_{[a,b]}
obtained from the identity by reversing letters
[a,b]
in positions
[a,b]
, and ensuring that the resulting permutation belongs to
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
.
To be precise, we describe such elements using three cases:
a=b
,
a=\overline{b}
(
b>0
), and
0<a<b
. When
a=b
, we have the trivial reversal
s_{[b,b]}=s_{\emptyset}=e
. For
b>0
, the reversal
s_{[\overline{b},b]}
is the element having one-line notation

(2.9)

\overline{n}\cdots\overline{(b+1)}\cdot b\cdots 1\cdot\overline{1}\cdots\overline{b}\cdot(b+1)\cdots n,

and equal to the product of generators
s_{0}(s_{1}s_{0}s_{1})(s_{2}s_{1}s_{0}s_{1}s_{2})\cdots(s_{b-1}\dotsc s_{1}s_{0}s_{1}\dotsc s_{b-1})
. For
0<a<b
the reversal
s_{[a,b]}
is the element having
one-line notation

\overline{n}\cdots\overline{(b+1)}\cdot\overline{a}\cdots\overline{b}\cdot\overline{(a-1)}\cdots\overline{1}\cdot 1\cdots(a-1)\cdot b\cdots a\cdot(b+1)\cdots n,

and equal to the product of generators
s_{a}(s_{a+1}s_{a})(s_{a+2}s_{a+1}s_{a})\cdots(s_{b-1}\cdots s_{a})
. Each reversal
s_{[a,b]}
is the unique element of maximum length in a parabolic subgroup of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
, generated by

(2.10)

J_{[a,b]}:=\begin{cases}\{s_{0},\dotsc,s_{b-1}\}&\text{if $a=\overline{b}$ ($b>0$)},\\
\{s_{a},\dotsc,s_{b-1}\}&\text{if $0<a<b$}.\end{cases}

The first equality in (
2.2
) implies that when
J=J_{[a,b]}
, the subset
W^{J}_{-}
of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
may be characterized in terms
of long one-line notation. Specifically, we have the following.

Lemma 2.3
.

For
J=J_{[a,b]}
, each minimum-length coset representative
w\in W^{J}_{-}
satisfies

(i)

w_{\overline{b}}^{-1}<\cdots<w_{\overline{a}}^{-1}
and
w_{a}^{-1}<\cdots<w_{b}^{-1}
for
a>0
,

(ii)

w_{\overline{b}}^{-1}<\cdots<w_{\overline{1}}^{-1}<w_{1}^{-1}<\cdots<w_{b}^{-1}
for
a=\overline{b}
.

Proof.

By
(
2.1
) and
the action preceding (
2.5
), we have the equivalences

ws_{i}>w\ \Longleftrightarrow\ s_{i}w^{-1}>w^{-1}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \begin{cases}w_{i}^{-1}<w_{i+1}^{-1}&\text{if $1\leq i\leq n-1$},\\
w_{\overline{1}}^{-1}<w_{1}^{-1}&\text{if $i=0$}.\end{cases}

Thus by (
2.2
), (
2.10
), the combination of conditions
(i)
,
(ii)

is equivalent to the minimality of
w
in

wW_{J}
.
∎

3.
The Hecke algebra of the hyperoctahedral group

Given Coxeter group
W
with generator set
S
, define
the
Hecke algebra

H=H(W)
of
W
to be
the
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\frac{1}{2}},q^{\negthinspace\bar{\,}\frac{1}{2}}]
-span of
\{T_{w}\,|\,w\in W\}

with unit
T_{e}
and multiplication defined by

(3.1)

T_{w}T_{s}=\begin{cases}qT_{ws}+(q-1)T_{w}&\text{if $ws<w$},\\
T_{ws}&\text{if $ws>w$},\end{cases}

where
s\in S
and
w\in W
. This formula guarantees that for
w\in W
and
any reduced expression
s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{\ell}}
for
w
, we have
T_{w}=\smash{T_{s_{i_{1}}}\cdots T_{s_{i_{\ell}}}}
.
Consequently, for
v,w\in W
we have the implication

(3.2)

\ell(v)+\ell(w)=\ell(vw)\quad\Rightarrow\quad T_{v}T_{w}=T_{vw}.

Call
\{T_{w}\,|\,w\in W\}
the
natural basis
of
H
.

A second basis
[
13
]

of
H
is the (rescaled)

Kazhdan–Lusztig basis

\{\widetilde{C}_{w}(q)\,|\,w\in W\}
,
related to the natural basis by

(3.3)

\widetilde{C}_{w}(q)=\sum_{v\leq w}P_{v,w}(q)T_{v},

where

\{P_{v,w}(q)\,|\,v,w\in W\}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}[q]
are the

Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials
,
whose recursive definition appears in
[
13
]
. Coefficients of these polynomials may be interpreted in terms of
intersection cohomology
\mathrm{IH}^{*}(\Omega_{w})

[
14
]
, where
\Omega_{w}
is a certain
Schubert variety
indexed by
w
. (See, e.g.,
[
2
]
.) In particular, when
\Omega_{w}
is rationally smooth, all polynomials
\{P_{v,w}(q)\,|\,v\leq w\}
are identically
1

[
13
, Thm. A.2]
.

For each subset
J
of generators of
W
, one forms a natural
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\frac{1}{2}},q^{\negthinspace\bar{\,}\frac{1}{2}}]
-submodule
of
H
by taking the span of sums

(3.4)

T_{uW_{J}}:=\negthickspace\sum_{v\in uW_{J}}\negthickspace T_{v}

of natural basis elements of
H
with each sum corresponding to a coset of
W_{J}
. This submodule

(3.5)

H_{J}:=\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{Z}[q^{\frac{1}{2}},q^{\negthinspace\bar{\,}\frac{1}{2}}]}\{T_{uW_{J}}\,|\,u\in W^{J}_{-}\}

in fact forms a left
H
-module. A nice formula for the action of
H
on
H_{J}
was given by Douglass
[
9
, Prop. 2.3]
.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
s
be a generator of
W
, and let
J

be some subset of the generators. Then for all
w\in W
, we have

(3.6)

T_{s}T_{wW_{J}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}qT_{swW_{J}}+(q-1)T_{wW_{J}}&\text{if }swW_{J}<wW_{J},\\
T_{swW_{J}}&\text{if }swW_{J}>wW_{J},\\
qT_{wW_{J}}&\text{if }swW_{J}=wW_{J}.\end{array}\right.

Corollary 3.2
.

If
v\in W_{J}
, then

T_{v}T_{W_{J}}=q^{\ell(v)}T_{W_{J}}
.

Let us consider the type-
\mathsf{BC}
Hecke algebra
H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
, generated by

T_{s_{0}},\dotsc,T_{s_{n-1}}
subject to relations

(3.7)

\displaystyle T_{s_{i}}^{2}

\displaystyle=(q-1)T_{s_{i}}+qT_{e}

\displaystyle\text{for $i=0,\dotsc,n-1$,}

\displaystyle T_{s_{0}}T_{s_{1}}T_{s_{0}}T_{s_{1}}

\displaystyle=T_{s_{1}}T_{s_{0}}T_{s_{1}}T_{s_{0}},

\displaystyle T_{s_{i}}T_{s_{j}}

\displaystyle=T_{s_{j}}T_{s_{i}}

\displaystyle\text{for $i,j\geq 0$ and $|i-j|\geq 2$,}

\displaystyle T_{s_{i}}T_{s_{j}}T_{s_{i}}

\displaystyle=T_{s_{j}}T_{s_{i}}T_{s_{j}}

\displaystyle\text{for $i,j\geq 1$ and $|i-j|=1$,}

a subset
J=J_{[a,b]}
of generators of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
for some
a<b
, and the submodule
H_{J}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q):=H_{J}
of
H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
. By
[
2
, §13.3.7]
,
rational smoothness of Schubert varieties of types
\mathsf{A}
,
\mathsf{B}
,
\mathsf{C}

can be characterized in terms of pattern avoidance. In particular, when
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoids the patterns
3412
and
4231
, we have the stronger property
that type-
\mathsf{B}
and
\mathsf{C}
Schubert varieties indexed by
w
are both smooth. Since a reversal in
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoids both of these patterns, we have the following.

Proposition 3.3
.

For each reversal
s_{[a,b]}\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
,
we have

\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a,b]}}(q)=\sum_{v\leq s_{[a,b]}}T_{v}.

Each Kazhdan–Lusztig basis element indexed by a reversal
s_{[a,b]}
is itself a coset sum (
3.4
) for the parabolic subgroup generated by
J=J_{[a.b]}
:

(3.8)

T_{W_{J}}=\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a,b]}}(q).

By (
2.3
), other defining basis elements (
3.5
) of
H_{J}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
can be written as

(3.9)

T_{uW_{J}}=T_{u}\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a,b]}}(q),

for
u\in W^{J}_{-}
.
Thus by Proposition
3.1
, each product of the form

(3.10)

\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}}(q)\cdots\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{k},b_{k}]}}(q)

belongs to
H^{\mathsf{BC}}_{J_{[a_{k},b_{k}]}}(q)
and we can expand it in the defining basis of this module as follows.

Proposition 3.4
.

Suppose that the product of the first
k-1
factors of (
3.10
)
expands in the natural basis of
H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)

as

(3.11)

\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}}(q)\cdots\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{k-1},b_{k-1}]}}(q)=\sum_{v\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}}c_{v}T_{v},

for some polynomials
\{c_{v}=c_{v}(q)\,|\,v\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\}
in
\mathbb{Z}[q]
,
and define
J=J_{[a_{k},b_{k}]}
. Then the full product (
3.10
)
expands in the defining basis of
H_{J}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
as

(3.12)

\sum_{w\in W^{J}_{-}}\negthickspace\bigg(\sum_{u\in W_{J}}\negthinspace q^{\ell(u)}c_{wu}\bigg)T_{w}\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{k},b_{k}]}}(q).

Proof.

Using
(
2.3
) to factor each element
v
on the right-hand side of
(
3.11
) as
v=wu
with
w\in W^{J}_{-}
,
u\in W_{J}
,
we may express the product (
3.10
) as

\sum_{w\in W^{J}_{-}}\negthickspace\bigg(\sum_{u\in W_{J}}\negthickspace c_{wu}T_{w}T_{u}\bigg)\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{k},b_{k}]}}(q).

Then by Corollary
3.2
,
we have
T_{u}\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{k},b_{k}]}}(q)=q^{\ell(u)}\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{k},b_{k}]}}(q)
and the claimed formula.
∎

4.
Type-
\mathsf{BC}
star networks

To graphically represent products of the form (
3.10
),
we extend the idea of type-
\mathsf{BC}
wiring
diagrams to include planar networks in which interior vertices may have indegrees and outdegrees greater than 2. In particular, we associate to each reversal
s_{[a,b]}\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
a
type-
\mathsf{BC}
simple star network

\smash{F_{[a,b]}}
having
2n
source vertices on the left and
2n
sink vertices on the right, both labeled
\overline{n},\dotsc,n
from bottom to top. For the three cases
a=b
,
a=\overline{b}
(
b>0
),
0<a<b
of reversals,
we include edges and
0
,
1
, or
2
additional
interior vertices as follows.

(1)

When
a=b
,

\smash{F_{[b,b]}=F_{\emptyset}}
has a directed edge from source
i
to sink
i
, for
i=\overline{n},\dotsc,n
.

(2)

When
a=\overline{b}
(
b>0
),
F_{[a,b]}
has one interior vertex. For
i=a,\dotsc,b
, a directed edge begins at source
i
and terminates at the interior vertex, and another directed edge begins at the interior vertex and terminates at sink
i
.

(3)

When
0<a<b
,
F_{[a,b]}
has two interior vertices, which we call
upper
and
lower
. For
i=a,\dotsc,b
, a directed edge begins at source
i
and terminates at the upper interior vertex, and another directed edge begins at the upper interior vertex and terminates at sink
i
. For
i=\overline{b},\dotsc,\overline{a}
, a directed edge begins at source
i
and terminates at the lower interior vertex, and another directed edge begins at the lower interior vertex and terminates at sink
i
.

For economy of figures, we omit vertices and left-to-right orientations of edges. For example, the seven type-
\mathsf{BC}
simple star networks

(4.1)

{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

correspond to the reversals

s_{\emptyset}=e
,
s_{[\overline{1},1]}
,
s_{[\overline{2},2]}
,
s_{[\overline{3},3]}
,
s_{[1,2]}
,
s_{[2,3]}
,
s_{[1,3]}
in
\mathfrak{B}_{3}
.
Traditional
type-
\mathsf{BC}
wiring diagrams
are formed by concatenating networks

F_{[\overline{1},1]},F_{[1,2]},\dotsc,F_{[n-1,n]}

indexed by intervals of cardinality
2
. More generally, define
type-
\mathsf{BC}
star networks
to be the set
\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{BC}}(n)
of all concatenations

(4.2)

F_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}\circ\cdots\circ F_{[a_{k},b_{k}]}

of type-
\mathsf{BC}
simple star networks,
where

F_{[a_{i},b_{i}]}\circ F_{[a_{i+1},b_{i+1}]}
is formed, informally, by identifying all sink vertices of
F_{[a_{i},b_{i}]}
with the corresponding source vertices of
F_{[a_{i+1},b_{i+1}]}
. More precisely,
for all
j
we replace the unique edge
(x,\mathrm{sink}\;j)
of
F_{[a_{i},b_{i}]}
and the unique
edge
(\mathrm{source}\;j,y)
of
F_{[a_{i+1},b_{i+1}]}

by a single edge
(x,y)

in
F_{[a_{i},b_{i}]}\circ F_{[a_{i+1},b_{i+1}]}
. Again for economy, we omit vertices and edge orientations in the resulting directed multigraph. For instance, the type-
\mathsf{BC}
star network
F_{[1,3]}\circ F_{[2,3]}\circ F_{[1,2]}\circ F_{[\overline{1},1]}\in\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{BC}}(3)
, drawn once economically without vertices and edge orientations and once with all details, is

(4.3)

\lxSVG@closescope

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\negthinspace.

All networks (
4.2
) in
\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{BC}}(n)
can be drawn to exhibit mirror symmetry in a horizontal line separating the positively indexed sources and sinks from the negatively indexed sources and sinks. Alluding to this symmetry, we will refer to sources
j
and
\overline{j}
, sinks
j
and
\overline{j}
, and central vertices of the upper and lower stars of
F_{[a_{i},b_{i}]}
(for
a_{i}>0
) in (
4.2
) as
reflections
of one another. The central vertex of
F_{[\overline{b},b]}
is its own reflection. Similarly, we will refer to corresponding pairs of edges as reflections of one another.

Following
[
3
]
,
[
6
]
, we
consider families

\pi=(\pi_{\overline{n}},\dotsc,\pi_{\overline{1}},\pi_{1},\dotsc,\pi_{n})

of paths
covering
a star network
F\in\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{BC}}(n)
, i.e., using all edges in
F
. Call
\pi
a
\mathsf{BC}
-path family
if
for each index
i\in[1,n]
,
the reflections of the edges forming path
\pi_{i}
are precisely the edges forming path
\pi_{\overline{i}}
. That is,
\pi_{\overline{i}}
must be the reflection of
\pi_{i}
.
Define
\pi
to have
type
u=u_{\overline{n}}\cdots u_{\overline{1}}u_{1}\cdots u_{n}\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
if for all
i
, path
\pi_{i}
begins at source
i
and terminates at sink
u_{i}
. In this case, we write
\mathrm{sink}(\pi_{i})=u_{i}
, or
\mathrm{sink}_{F}(\pi_{i})=u_{i}
if there is any danger of confusion. For
F\in\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{BC}}(n)
and
u\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
, define the sets

(4.4)

\begin{gathered}\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(F)=\{\pi\,|\,\pi\text{ a $\mathsf{BC}$-path family covering }F\},\\
\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{u}(F)=\{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(F)\,|\,\mathrm{type}(\pi)=u\}.\end{gathered}

(4.5)

covering
F:=F_{[1,3]}\circ F_{[2,3]}\circ F_{[1,2]}\circ F_{[\overline{1},1]}

(
4.3
)
satisfy

\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{123}(F)
,
\sigma\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{3\overline{1}2}(F)
,
\tau\not\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(F)
, with
\tau
failing to be a
\mathsf{BC}
-path family because
\tau_{1},\tau_{\overline{1}}
in blue (also
\tau_{2},\tau_{\overline{2}}
in red) are not reflections of one another.

By Lemma
2.2
, we may interpret

\ell(u)
for all path families in
\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{u}(F)
as follows.

Observation 4.1
.

Within
each path family
\pi=(\pi_{\overline{n}},\dotsc,\pi_{\overline{1}},\pi_{1},\dotsc,\pi_{n})\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{u}(F)
, there are

\ell(u)
pairs
(\pi_{i},\pi_{j})
of paths satisfying
|i|<j
and
\mathrm{sink}(\pi_{j})<\mathrm{sink}(\pi_{i})
.

For example, in the path family
\sigma\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{3\overline{1}2}(F)
in (
4.5
),
the
\ell(3\overline{1}2)=3
pairs of paths satisfying
the conditions of the observation are
(\sigma_{\overline{2}},\sigma_{2})
,
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{3})
, and
(\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3})
.

Each element
F\in\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{BC}}(n)
combinatorially interprets an element in
H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
. To describe this interpretation, we extend the defect statistic
[
3
]
,
[
6
]
,
[
8
]
described in Section
1
, using the inversions appearing in Lemma
2.2
. Given a
\mathsf{BC}
-path family
\pi
covering
F
, define a
type-
\mathsf{BC}
defect
of
\pi
to be
a triple
(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)
with
|i|\leq j
, and
\pi_{i}
,
\pi_{j}
meeting at one of the internal vertices of
F_{[c_{k},d_{k}]}
after having crossed an odd number of times. Let
\mathrm{dfct}^{\mathsf{BC}}(\pi)
denote the number of type-
\mathsf{BC}
defects of
\pi
.
We say that
F\in\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{\mathsf{BC}}}(n)

graphically represents

(4.6)

\sum_{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(F)}\negthickspace\negthickspace q^{\mathrm{dfct}^{\mathsf{BC}}(\pi)}T_{\mathrm{type}(\pi)}

as an element of
H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
. Refining the sets (
4.4
) by counting defects of path families in them, let us define

(4.7)

\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{u,d}(F)=\{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{u}(F)\,|\,\mathrm{dfct}^{\mathsf{BC}}(\pi)=d\}.

Using this notation we may express (
4.6
) as

\sum_{u\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}}\sum_{d\geq 0}|\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{u,d}(F)|q^{d}T_{u}.

{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -29.53 L 19.69 29.53 L 39.37 29.53 L 59.06 9.84 L 68.9 0 L 78.74 9.84}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

We have
\mathrm{dfct}^{\mathsf{BC}}(\pi)=4
: defects of
\pi
are
(\pi_{\overline{1}},\pi_{1},2)
,
(\pi_{\overline{1}},\pi_{2},3)
,
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2},4)
,
(\pi_{\overline{2}},\pi_{2},4)
.

5.
Main results

When one compares a concatenation
F^{\prime}
of
k-1
simple star networks and a concatenation
F
of
F^{\prime}
with one more simple star network, one sees bijections between certain sets of path families in
F
and
F^{\prime}
.

Proposition 5.1
.

Fix a sequence
(s_{[a_{1},b_{1}]},\dotsc,s_{[a_{k},b_{k}]})

of reversals in
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
, define the generator subset
J=J_{[a_{k},b_{k}]}
,
and choose an element
w\in W^{J}_{-}
.
Consider two type-
\mathsf{BC}
star networks

F^{\prime}=F_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}\circ\cdots\circ F_{[a_{k-1},b_{k-1}]}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad F=F^{\prime}\circ F_{[a_{k},b_{k}]}.

Then for each element
v\in W_{J}
and each
d\geq 0
, we have a bijection

(5.1)

\Pi_{wv,d}(F)\quad\overset{1-1}{\longleftrightarrow}\ \ \bigcup_{u\in W_{J}}\Pi_{wu\negthinspace,\;d-\ell(u)}(F^{\prime}).

Proof.

Fix
w
,
J
as in the proposition.
We demonstrate a bijection (
5.1
)
in the case that
v=e
. Observe that each path family
\pi
in
\Pi_{w}(F)
can be decomposed uniquely as the concatenation of its truncation
\pi^{\prime}
to
F^{\prime}
with its truncation
\pi^{\prime\prime}
to
F_{[a_{k},b_{k}]}
: for some
u\in W_{J}
we have

(5.2)

\pi=\pi^{\prime}\circ\pi^{\prime\prime},\qquad\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi_{wu}(F^{\prime}),\qquad\pi^{\prime\prime}\in\Pi_{u^{-1}}(F_{[a_{k},b_{k}]}).

Let us write the first truncation map
\pi\mapsto\pi^{\prime}
with
w
unspecified as
\mathrm{trunc}\negthinspace:\Pi(F)\rightarrow\Pi(F^{\prime})
, and let us use
\psi
to denote the restriction of
\mathrm{trunc}
to
\Pi_{w}(F)
,

(5.3)

\psi:\Pi_{w}(F)\rightarrow\bigcup_{u\in W_{J}}\Pi_{wu}(F^{\prime}).

We claim that
\psi
is bijective. To see this, choose a path family
\tau
belonging to
\cup_{u\in W_{J}}\Pi_{wu}(F^{\prime})
and let
y=y(\tau)
be the element of
W_{J}
such that
\mathrm{type}(\tau)=wy
. The set
\Pi_{y^{-1}}(F_{[a_{k},b_{k}]})
contains a unique element, call it
\sigma_{y^{-1}}
.
Now define a map

(5.4)

\displaystyle\kappa:\bigcup_{u\in W_{J}}\Pi_{wu}(F^{\prime})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Pi_{w}(F)

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle\mapsto\tau\circ\sigma_{y(\tau)^{-1}}.

It is easy to see that
\kappa
inverts
\psi
.

Now we claim that for
\pi\in\Pi_{w}(F)
, the path families
\pi
,
\pi^{\prime}=\psi(\pi)
satisfy

\mathrm{dfct}(\pi^{\prime})=\mathrm{dfct}(\pi)-\ell(u).

To see this, recall that by Observation
4.1
,
\pi
contains
\ell(w)
pairs
(\pi_{i},\pi_{j})
with
|i|<j
and
\mathrm{sink}(\pi_{j})<\mathrm{sink}(\pi_{i})
. By Lemma
2.3
, the condition
w\in W^{J}_{-}
implies that the numbers
\mathrm{sink}(\pi_{j})
and
\mathrm{sink}(\pi_{i})
cannot both belong to the interval
[a_{k},b_{k}]

(or to the interval
[\overline{b_{k}},\overline{a_{k}}]
if
a_{k}>0
). Similarly, by (
2.3
),
\pi^{\prime}
contains
\ell(wu)=\ell(w)+\ell(u)

pairs
(\pi^{\prime}_{i},\pi^{\prime}_{j})
with
|i|<j
and

(5.5)

\mathrm{sink}_{F^{\prime}}(\pi^{\prime}_{j})<\mathrm{sink}_{F^{\prime}}(\pi^{\prime}_{i}).

Thus the concatenation
\pi=\pi^{\prime}\circ\pi^{\prime\prime}
extends exactly

\ell(u)
such pairs to paths
(\pi_{i},\pi_{j})
which cross in
F_{[a_{k},b_{k}]}
and by Lemma
2.3
satisfy

(5.6)

\mathrm{sink}_{F}(\pi_{i})<\mathrm{sink}_{F}(\pi_{j}).

Comparing (
5.5
) and (
5.6
), we see
that these crossings are defective and contribute
\ell(u)
defects to
\pi
beyond
the defects present in
\pi^{\prime}
.

We complete the proof of the bijections (
5.1
) by
demonstrating bijections between all pairs of sets
\Pi_{wv,d}(F)
and
\Pi_{wy,d}(F)
with
v,y\in W_{J}
. In particular, for fixed
v,y\in W_{J}
, define the map
\phi_{v,y}:\Pi_{wv}(F)\rightarrow\Pi_{wy}(F)
by writing
\pi=\mathrm{trunc}(\pi)\circ\sigma_{z^{-1}}
for some
z\in W_{J}
and mapping

(5.7)

\pi\mapsto\mathrm{trunc}(\pi)\circ\sigma_{z^{-1}v^{-1}y}.

To see that
\phi_{v,y}
is well defined, observe that since the type of
\mathrm{trunc}(\pi)
is
wvz
, the type of
\phi_{v,y}(\pi)
is
wvzz^{-1}v^{-1}y=wy
. To see that
\phi_{v,y}
is invertible, observe that its inverse is
\phi_{y,v}
:

\displaystyle\phi_{y,v}(\phi_{v,y}(\pi))

\displaystyle=\phi_{y,v}(\mathrm{trunc}(\pi)\circ\sigma_{z^{-1}v^{-1}y})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{trunc}(\pi)\circ\sigma_{(z^{-1}v^{-1}y)y^{-1}v}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{trunc}(\pi)\circ\sigma_{z^{-1}}

\displaystyle=\pi.

Finally, we claim that
\phi_{v,y}
restricts to a bijection from
\Pi_{wv,d}(F)
to
\Pi_{wy,d}(F)
for all
d
. To see this, observe that as
z
varies over
W_{J}
, the set of paths of
\mathrm{trunc}(\pi)\circ\sigma_{z^{-1}}
which meet at the central vertex of
F_{[a_{k-1},b_{k-1}]}
is constant, regardless of whether they cross there. Thus
\mathrm{dfct}^{\mathsf{BC}}(\pi^{\prime}\circ\sigma_{z^{-1}})
is also constant, and
\phi_{v}
restricts to a bijection from
\Pi_{w,d}(F)
to
\Pi_{wv,d}(F)
.
∎

This leads to our main result:
the star network
F_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}\circ\cdots\circ F_{[a_{k},b_{k}]}
graphically represents the product
\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}}(q)\cdots\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{k},b_{k}]}}(q)
in the sense of (
4.6
).

Theorem 5.2
.

Fix a sequence
(s_{[a_{1},b_{1}]},\dotsc,s_{[a_{k},b_{k}]})

of reversals in
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
and define the type-
\mathsf{BC}
star network

(5.8)

F=F_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}\circ\cdots\circ F_{[a_{k},b_{k}]}.

Then we have

(5.9)

\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}}(q)\cdots\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{k},b_{k}]}}(q)=\sum_{y\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}}\sum_{d\geq 0}|\Pi_{y,d}(F)|q^{d}T_{y}.

Proof.

For any generator subset of the form
J=J_{[a,b]}
, we have by Proposition
3.3
that the coefficient of
T_{u}
in
\smash{\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a,b]}}(q)}
is
1
for
u\in W_{J}
and is
0
otherwise.
On the other hand, the star network
F_{[a,b]}
satisfies

|\Pi_{u,d}(F_{[a,b]})|=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $u\in W_{J}$ and $d=0$},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Thus the identity (
5.9
) holds for a single Kazhdan–Lusztig basis element indexed by a reversal.
Suppose therefore that the identity holds for products of
1,\dotsc,k-1

such elements and consider a product of
k
of them. Writing
F^{\prime}=F_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}\circ\cdots\circ F_{[a_{k-1},b_{k-1}]}
, we have

(5.10)

\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}}(q)\cdots\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{k-1},b_{k-1}]}}(q)=\sum_{v\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}}\sum_{d\geq 0}|\Pi_{v,d}(F^{\prime})|q^{d}T_{v}.

Defining
J=J_{[a_{k},b_{k}]}
and applying Proposition
3.4
to (
5.9
) – (
5.10
) we have that the left-hand side of (
5.9
) equals

(5.11)

\sum_{w\in W^{J}_{-}}\negthickspace\bigg(\sum_{u\in W_{J}}\sum_{d\geq 0}|\Pi_{wu,d}(F^{\prime})|q^{\ell(u)+d}\bigg)T_{w}\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{k},b_{k}]}}(q).

Since
\Pi_{wu,c}(F^{\prime})=\emptyset
when
c<0
, the parenthesized expression in (
5.11
) equals

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in W_{\negthinspace J}}\sum_{\ d\geq-\ell(u)}\negthickspace\negthickspace|\Pi_{wu,d}(F^{\prime})|q^{\ell(u)+d}

\displaystyle=\sum_{u\in W_{J}}\sum_{d\geq 0}|\Pi_{wu,d-\ell(u)}(F^{\prime})|q^{d}

\displaystyle=\sum_{d\geq 0}q^{d}\sum_{u\in W_{J}}|\Pi_{wu,d-\ell(u)}(F^{\prime})|.

By Proposition
5.1
, this simplifies to

\sum_{d\geq 0}q^{d}|\Pi_{w,d}(F)|.

Plugging this into (
5.11
) and writing
\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{k},b_{k}]}}(q)=\sum_{v\in W_{J}}T_{v}
, we have

(5.12)

\displaystyle\sum_{w\in W^{J}_{-}}\sum_{d\geq 0}q^{d}|\Pi_{w,d}(F)|T_{w}\negthickspace\sum_{v\in W_{J}}\negthickspace T_{v}=\sum_{w\in W^{J}_{-}}\sum_{v\in W_{J}}\sum_{d\geq 0}q^{d}|\Pi_{w,d}(F)|T_{w}T_{v}.

By Proposition
5.1
, we have
|\Pi_{w,d}(F)|=|\Pi_{wv,d}(F)|
for each
v\in W_{J}
. By (
2.3
) and (
3.2
),
the final expression in (
5.12
) is equal to the right-hand side of (
5.9
).
∎

As a consequence of Theorem
5.2
, we can prove a more general statement.
First let us extend the set
\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{\mathsf{BC}}}(n)
to include more general planar networks.
Observe that sometimes in a
concatenation
G\circ F
, there exist vertices
x
in
G
,
y
in
F
with
m(x,y)>1

edges incident upon both. Define the
condensed concatenation

G\bullet F
to be the subdigraph
of
G\circ F
obtained by removing, for all such pairs
(x,y)
, all but one of the
m(x,y)
edges
incident upon both, and by marking this edge with the multiplicity
m(x,y)
.
Let
\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{\mathsf{BC}}}_{\bullet}(n)
denote
the union of simple star networks in
\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{\mathsf{BC}}}(n)
and all condensed concatenations of these. Let
\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{\mathsf{BC}}}_{\bullet,\circ}(n)
denote
the union of
\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{\mathsf{BC}}}_{\bullet}(n)
and the set of ordinary concatenations of these. Call
\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{\mathsf{BC}}}_{\bullet,\circ}(n)
the set of
generalized star networks
, and observe that we have
\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{\mathsf{BC}}}(n)\subset\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{\mathsf{BC}}}_{\bullet,\circ}(n)
.

For
F\in\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{\mathsf{BC}}}_{\bullet,\circ}(n)
, we define
the set
\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(F)
of
\mathsf{BC}
-path families coverin
F
as before, with the additional requirement that
for each edge of multiplicity
k
in
F
,
a path family
\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(F)
must
contain
k
paths
\pi_{i_{1}},\dotsc,\pi_{i_{k}}
which include the given edge.
For example, one network in
the set
\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{\mathsf{BC}}}_{\bullet,\circ}(2)
and one path family
covering it are

(5.13)

F_{[\overline{2},2]}\circ F_{[\overline{1},1]}\circ F_{[1,2]}\bullet F_{[\overline{2},2]}=\hbox to79.95pt{\vbox to54.44pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 11.52347pt\lower-27.51146pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

(Compare (
5.13
) to (
4.8
).)

In
[
16
, Thm. 5.21]
it was proved that for all
v\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
,
the Kazhdan–Lusztig basis element
\widetilde{C}_{v}(q)
of
H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
is graphically represented by a network
F_{v}
in
\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{\mathsf{BC}}}_{\bullet}(n)
.

Proposition 5.3
.

For each element
v\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
,
there exists a network

(5.14)

F_{v}=F_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}\bullet\cdots\bullet F_{[a_{p},b_{p}]}

in
\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{\mathsf{BC}}}_{\bullet}(n)
satisfying

\widetilde{C}_{v}(q)=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(F_{v})}q^{\mathrm{dfct}^{\mathsf{BC}}(\pi)}T_{\mathrm{type}(\pi)}=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(F_{v})}T_{\mathrm{type}(\pi)}.

In particular, for all
u\leq v
, the set
\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(F_{v})
contains one path family of type
u
and this path family is defect-free. Furthermore,
at most one interval
[a_{i},b_{i}]
in (
5.14
) satisfies
a_{i}=\overline{b_{i}},
and if such an interval exists, we may assume that
i=1
or
i=p
.

The symmetry of elementary star networks of type
\mathsf{BC}
guarantees that in the network
F_{v}
(
5.14
), any edge labeled with a multiplicity has a reflection labeled with the same multiplicity. Assume that there are
2p=2p(v)
such edges,
and let the multiplicities of those edges which are incident upon at least one central vertex of an upper star of an
elementary star network in (
5.14
) be

(5.15)

m_{v,1},\dotsc,m_{v,p(v)}.

These multiplicities and the
q
-analogs of their factorials relate the Kazhdan–Lusztig basis element
\widetilde{C}_{v}(q)
to the network

(5.16)

G_{v}:=F_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}\circ\cdots\circ F_{[a_{p},b_{p}]}

in
\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{\mathsf{BC}}}(n)
defined in terms of
F_{v}
(
5.14
).
Recalling the standard definitions

[n]_{q}:=\begin{cases}1+q+\cdots+q^{n-1}&\text{if $n\geq 1$},\\
0&\text{if $n=0$},\end{cases}\qquad[n]_{q}!:=\begin{cases}[n]_{q}\cdots[1]_{q}&\text{if $n\geq 1$},\\
1&\text{if $n=0$},\end{cases}

and defining

(5.17)

r(v):=[m_{v,1}]_{q}!\cdots[m_{v,p(v)}]_{q}!,

in terms of (
5.15
), we have the following
[
15
, Thm. 4.3]
.

Lemma 5.4
.

For each element
v\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
,
the type-
\mathsf{BC}
star network
G_{v}
satisfies

(5.18)

r(v)\widetilde{C}_{v}(q)=\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}}(q)\cdots\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{p},b_{p}]}}(q)=\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(G_{v})}\negthickspace\negthickspace q^{\mathrm{dfct}^{\mathsf{BC}}(\pi)}T_{\mathrm{type}(\pi)}.

In other words, the replacement of
G_{v}
by
F_{v}
on the right-hand side of (
5.18
) leads to the equation

(5.19)

\sum_{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(G_{v})}\negthickspace\negthickspace q^{\mathrm{dfct}^{\mathsf{BC}}(\pi)}T_{\mathrm{type}(\pi)}=r(v)\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(F_{v})}\negthickspace\negthickspace q^{\mathrm{dfct}^{\mathsf{BC}}(\pi)}T_{\mathrm{type}(\pi)}.

More generally, for any generalized star networks
G
and
F
, with
F
formed by replacing multiple edges in
G
by a single edge in
F
,
path families in
F
and
G
are related in the following simple way.

Lemma 5.5
.

Fix a generalized star network
G
in which
m
multiplicity-
1
edges are incident upon a pair
\{x,y\}
of vertices and the
m
reflections of these edges are incident upon the reflections
\{\overline{x},\overline{y}\}
of
x
and
y
. Assume that at most one of the vertices
\{x,y\}
equals its reflection. Replace the edges incident upon
x
and
y

by a single multiplicity-
m
edge,
replace their reflections by another such edge, and call the resulting generalized star network
F
.
Then we have

(5.20)

\sum_{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(G)}\negthickspace\negthickspace q^{\mathrm{dfct}^{\mathsf{BC}}(\pi)}T_{\mathrm{type}(\pi)}=[m]_{q}!\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(F)}\negthickspace\negthickspace q^{\mathrm{dfct}^{\mathsf{BC}}(\pi)}T_{\mathrm{type}(\pi)}.

Proof.

Assume that
x
and
y
lie on or above the horizontal line of symmetry in
G
. Let
\mathcal{E}=\{\varepsilon_{1},\dotsc,\varepsilon_{m}\}
be the set of
m
edges from
x
to
y
, labeled from bottom to top, and let
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
be the set of reflections of these.

Consider a path family
\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(G)
with the property that
edges
\varepsilon_{1},\dotsc,\varepsilon_{m}
belong to
paths
\pi_{i_{1}},\dotsc,\pi_{i_{m}}
,
respectively, with
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{m}
.
Define

\Phi(\pi,\mathcal{E})
to be the set of all path families in
\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(G)
which agree with
\pi
on every edge of
G
, except possibly for those edges belonging to
\mathcal{E}\cup\overline{\mathcal{E}}
.
Since each path family
\tau\in\Phi(\pi,\mathcal{E})

can differ from
\pi

only in that
edges
\epsilon_{1},\dotsc,\epsilon_{m}
belong to
paths
\tau_{u_{1}},\dotsc,\tau_{u_{m}}
, respectively,
for some permutation
u_{1}\cdots u_{m}
of
\{i_{1},\dotsc,i_{m}\}
, we have that

\mathrm{type}(\tau)=\mathrm{type}(\pi)
and

|\Phi(\pi,\mathcal{E})|=m!
.

We claim that each path family
\tau\in\Phi(\pi,\mathcal{E})
has defects which agree with those of
\pi
except possibly at the vertex
y
, where
\pi
has no defect:

(5.21)

\mathrm{dfct}^{\mathsf{BC}}(\tau)=\mathrm{dfct}^{\mathsf{BC}}(\pi)+\#\{\text{defects of $\tau$ occurring at vertex $y$}\}.

To see this, observe that our choice of
\pi
implies that each pair of paths among
\pi_{i_{1}},\dotsc,\pi_{i_{m}}
crosses an even number of times before meeting at
y
. Also the source-to-
x
subpaths of
\pi_{i_{1}},\dotsc,\pi_{i_{m}}
and
\tau_{i_{1}},\dotsc,\tau_{i_{m}}
are identical. Now any vertex
z
to the right of
y

belongs to
paths
\tau_{k}
,
\tau_{l}
if and only if it belongs to
\pi_{k},\pi_{l}
.
Furthermore, if
(\pi_{k},\pi_{l})
and
(\tau_{k},\tau_{l})
appear in the same relative order between
x
and
y
, then the number of crossings of
(\pi_{k},\pi_{l})
preceding
z
equals the number of crossings of
(\tau_{k},\tau_{l})
preceding
z
. Otherwise these numbers of crossings differ by
2
, with one pair crossing at
x
and at
y
while the other pair crosses at neither of these vertices.
In either case, the meeting of
(\pi_{k},\pi_{l})
at
z
is defective if and only if the meeting of
(\tau_{k},\tau_{l})
at
z
is defective.

Consider therefore the defects occurring at vertex
y
. By our choice of
i_{1},\dotsc,i_{m}
, the number of defects of
\tau
occurring at vertex
y
equals
\textsc{inv}(u_{1}\cdots u_{m})
. Thus by (
5.21
) we have

(5.22)

\sum_{\tau\in\Phi(\pi,\mathcal{E})}q^{\mathrm{dfct}^{\mathsf{BC}}(\tau)}=q^{\mathrm{dfct}^{\mathsf{BC}}(\pi)}\negthickspace\negthickspace\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{u\in\mathfrak{S}_{\{i_{1},\dotsc,i_{m}\}}}\negthickspace\negthickspace\negthickspace\negthickspace q^{\textsc{inv}(u)}=[m]_{q}!q^{\mathrm{dfct}^{\mathsf{BC}}(\pi)},

and the coefficient of
T_{w}
on left-hand-side of (
5.20
) equals

(5.23)

\sum_{\tau\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{w}(G)}\negthickspace\negthickspace q^{\mathrm{dfct}^{\mathsf{BC}}(\tau)}=\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{w}(G)\\
\text{having no }\\
\text{defect at }y\end{subarray}}\sum_{\tau\in\Phi(\pi,\mathcal{E})}q^{\mathrm{dfct}^{\mathsf{BC}}(\tau)}=[m]_{q}!\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{w}(G)\\
\text{having no }\\
\text{defect at }y\end{subarray}}\negthickspace\negthickspace q^{\mathrm{dfct}^{\mathsf{BC}}(\pi)}.

Now create network
F
from
G
by deleting edges
\varepsilon_{2},\dotsc,\varepsilon_{n}
,
\overline{\varepsilon_{2}},\dotsc,\overline{\varepsilon_{n}}
, and by assigning multiplicity
m
to
\varepsilon_{1}
and
\overline{\varepsilon_{1}}
.
Similarly, create path family
\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(F)
from
\pi
by replacing edges
\varepsilon_{2},\dotsc,\varepsilon_{m}
in
\pi_{i_{2}},\dotsc,\pi_{i_{m}}

with
\varepsilon_{1}
, and edges
\overline{\varepsilon_{2}},\dotsc,\overline{\varepsilon_{m}}

in
\pi_{\overline{i_{1}}},\dotsc,\pi_{\overline{i_{m}}}

with
\overline{\varepsilon_{1}}
. Specifically we have
\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{type}(\pi),\mathrm{dfct}(\pi)}(F)
. Moreover, it is easy to see that for each pair
(w,d)\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\times\mathbb{N}
, this procedure defines a bijective correspondence between the sets

\{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{w,d}(G)\,|\,\pi\text{ has no defect at }y\}
and
\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{w,d}(F)
. Thus we may rewrite the final sum in (
5.23
) as

[m]_{q}!\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{w}(F)}\negthickspace\negthickspace q^{\mathrm{dfct}^{\mathsf{BC}}(\pi)},

which is the coefficient of
T_{w}
on the right-hand-side of (
5.20
).
∎

Now we may extend Theorem
5.2
to sequences of elements of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
whose type-
\mathsf{B}
and type-
\mathsf{C}
Schubert varieties are simultaneously smooth.

Theorem 5.6
.

Fix a sequence
(v^{(1)},\dotsc,v^{(k)})
of
3412
-avoiding,
4231
-avoiding elements in
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
,
and define the generalized star network

(5.24)

F=F_{v^{(1)}}\circ\cdots\circ F_{v^{(k)}}.

Then we have

(5.25)

\widetilde{C}_{v^{(1)}}(q)\cdots\widetilde{C}_{v^{(k)}}(q)=\sum_{y\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}}\sum_{d\geq 0}|\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{y,d}(F)|q^{d}T_{y}.

Proof.

For
i=1,\dotsc,k
, write

E_{v^{(i)}}=F_{[a_{i,1},b_{i,1}]}\circ\cdots\circ F_{[a_{i,p_{i}},b_{i,p_{i}}]}

as in (
5.16
)
and define

r(v^{(i)})

as in (
5.15
), (
5.17
). Then define the ordinary type-
\mathsf{BC}
star network

(5.26)

E=E_{v^{(1)}}\circ\cdots\circ E_{v^{(k)}}.

By Theorem
5.2
and Lemma
5.4
, we have that

(5.27)

r(v^{(1)})\cdots r(v^{(k)})\widetilde{C}_{v^{(1)}}(q)\cdots\widetilde{C}_{v^{(k)}}(q)=\sum_{y\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}}\sum_{d\geq 0}|\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{y,d}(E)|q^{d}T_{y},

because both sides are equal to

(\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{1,1},b_{1,1}]}}(q)\cdots\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{1,p_{1}},b_{1,p_{1}}]}}(q))\quad\cdots\quad(\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{k,1},b_{k,1}]}}(q)\cdots\widetilde{C}_{s_{[a_{k,p_{k}},b_{k,p_{k}}]}}(q)).

By Lemma
5.5
, the right-hand side of (
5.27
) equals

r(v^{(1)})\cdots r(v^{(k)})\sum_{y\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}}\sum_{d\geq 0}|\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{y,d}(F)|q^{d}T_{y},

and we have the desired result.
∎

It is possible to show that Proposition
5.3
and Theorem
5.6
are not as strong as possible. In particular, consider
the elements
3412
,
4231
(in short one-line notation) of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
, which clearly do not avoid the patterns
3412
and
4231
.
Since
reduced expressions for these elements do not contain the generator
s_{0}
, the Kazhdan–Lusztig basis elements
\widetilde{C}_{3412}(q)
,
\widetilde{C}_{4231}(q)
factor just as their type-
\mathsf{A}
analogs do,

\widetilde{C}_{3412}(q)=\widetilde{C}_{s_{2}}(q)\widetilde{C}_{s_{1}}(q)\widetilde{C}_{s_{3}}(q)\widetilde{C}_{s_{2}}(q),\qquad\widetilde{C}_{4231}(q)=\widetilde{C}_{s_{1}}(q)\widetilde{C}_{s_{[2,4]}}(q)\widetilde{C}_{s_{1}}(q).

(The factorization of
\widetilde{C}_{3412}(q)
follows from
[
3
, Thm. 1]
; the factorization of
\widetilde{C}_{4231}(q)
is a straightforward computation, observed in
[
15
, §4]
.)
Now by Theorem
5.2
we may define networks

(5.28)

F_{3412}:=F_{[2,3]}\circ F_{[1,2]}\circ F_{[3,4]}\circ F_{[2,3]},\qquad F_{4231}:=F_{[1,2]}\circ F_{[2,4]}\circ F_{[1,2]}

which graphically represent
the Kazhdan–Lusztig basis elements

\widetilde{C}_{3412}(q)
,
\widetilde{C}_{4231}(q)
of
H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
,

\widetilde{C}_{3412}(q)=\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(F_{3412})}\negthickspace\negthickspace q^{\mathrm{dfct}^{\mathsf{BC}}(\pi)}T_{\mathrm{type}(\pi)},\qquad\widetilde{C}_{4231}(q)=\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(F_{4231})}\negthickspace\negthickspace q^{\mathrm{dfct}^{\mathsf{BC}}(\pi)}T_{\mathrm{type}(\pi)}.

Moreover, this gives positive combinatorial formulas for the corresponding sets of Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials,

P_{u,3412}(q)=\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{u}(F_{3412})}\negthickspace\negthickspace q^{\mathrm{dfct}^{\mathsf{BC}}(\pi)},\qquad P_{u,4231}(q)=\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{u}(F_{4231})}\negthickspace\negthickspace q^{\mathrm{dfct}^{\mathsf{BC}}(\pi)}.

Thus we have the following consequence of Theorem
5.2
.

Corollary 5.7
.

For
v\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
such that
\widetilde{C}_{v}(q)
is graphically represented by some network
F\in\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{\mathsf{BC}}}_{\bullet,\circ}(n)
, the Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials
\{P_{u,v}(q)\,|\,u\leq v\}
satisfy

P_{u,v}(q)=\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{u}(F)}\negthickspace\negthickspace q^{\mathrm{dfct}^{\mathsf{BC}}(\pi)}.

6.
Open problems

Corollary
5.7
suggests several problems.

Problem 6.1
.

Describe the elements
v\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
not avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231

for which
\widetilde{C}_{v}(q)
is graphically represented by
some network
F\in\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{\mathsf{BC}}}_{\bullet,\circ}(n)
.

For elements
v\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
having this property, the Kazhdan–Lusztig basis element
\widetilde{C}_{v}(q)
necessarily factors as a product of other Kazhdan–Lusztig basis elements indexed by reversals, possibly divided by a polynomial in
q
.

Problem 6.2
.

For
v
as in Problem
6.1
,
state an algorithm to construct
F_{v}
, equivalently, to factor
\widetilde{C}_{v}(q)
.

Preliminary results (e.g.,
[
7
]
) suggest that such factorizations do not exist for all
v\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
.

Problem 6.3
.

Prove that Kahzdan–Lusztig basis elements indexed by some subset of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
can not be graphically represented by generalized star networks.

It would also be interesting to extend the graphical interpretation of defects to Hecke algebras of Coxeter
groups of other types.

Problem 6.4
.

State and prove a type-
\mathsf{D}
analog of Theorem
5.2
.

Furthermore, perhaps
there is a nongraphical extension of Deodhar’s subexpressions and defects which
applies to Hecke algebras of Coxeter groups of all types.

Problem 6.5
.

Find a suitable extension of the defect statistic that applies to an arbitrary Coxeter group
W
,
so that for all sequences
(J_{1},\dotsc,J_{k})
of generator subsets of
W
, and maximal elements

v^{(1)},\dotsc,v^{(k)}
of

W_{J_{1}},\dotsc,W_{J_{k}}
, the coefficients in the expansion

\widetilde{C}_{v^{(1)}}(q)\cdots\widetilde{C}_{v^{(k)}}(q)=\sum_{w\in W}a_{w}T_{w}

may be interpreted as

a_{w}=a_{w}(q)=\sum_{\sigma}q^{\mathrm{dfct}(\sigma)},

where the sum is over sequences

\sigma=(\sigma_{1},\dotsc,\sigma_{k})\in W_{J_{1}}\times\cdots\times W_{J_{k}}
satisfying
\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{k}=w
.

7.
